ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e207	The proposal presented by the Progress Party on an enlarged and binding Norwegian railway investment plan is a proposal which is all the more reason to pay attention and debate. Transport policy and railway policy are an important means of achieving other overall societal objectives. The Committee has been gathered to stress that all people have a fundamental need for freedom, quality of life and a safe and secure everyday life. Businesses want the best possible assumptions to sell their products and services, and we others are going to work. These basic needs make it necessary to facilitate the rapid, efficient and safe transport of both persons and goods. The Committee has underlined and strongly expressed the fact that all accidents related to trains and crossings are a tragedy for all involved, and are, in this way, very unfortunate. The priority number one in railway operations must therefore always be safety. I'll just briefly mention that St.meld. No 16 for 2008-2009, National Transport Plan 2010–2019, provides a sound overview of planned infrastructure projects over the next decade. The problem is that it is not binding and predictable. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Progressive Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party, also point out that there is a significant weakness in the transport plan that this is precisely not mandatory and predictable and finds it unfortunate that the government is already in the first year of the planning period after the 2.3 billion ambitions. kr. The problems and deficiencies in the Norwegian railway system have become so serious and extensive over many years that there is a need for significant innovation and new solutions to ensure that we have a railway with life's right, even in the future. The punctuality in the railway sector has been steadily decreasing in the last five years, and that Norwegian railway is now in a critical phase, I think very many will stress. Within the railway there is a need for a strong improvement in capacity, quality, regularity and punctuality. Different actors in the railway sector point to a high need for increased appropriations, more predictability of funding, and that the development of rail tracks and tracks can take place at a significantly faster rate of investment than is currently the case. The majority of the Committee, the Right, the Progressive Party and the Christian People’s Party, believe that today’s critical railway state clearly demonstrates the need to re-evaluate today’s railway development funding mechanisms for both cost-benefit analysis, write-down rules and profitability requirements. It is important to arrange Norwegian rail through maintenance and development, regardless of who carries out the job, and regardless of who finances the development or maintenance. New financial instruments need to be considered in this way. An assessment must be made in all new major projects of what is the most appropriate organisational and funding solution for the project. The members of the Committee of the Workers' Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Centre are committed to ensuring stable and predictable appropriations for the railways, and they recall that less than a year ago, the Stortinget adopted a national transport plan. I expect that these parties will come back to their own views. So I'm going to talk about the Right View in particular. We are very concerned that the national transport plan needs to be of a significantly more general nature and be aimed at it. All modes of transport must be seen in the context of planning. The plan should contain different transport scenarios. We underline the need for binding action plans for Norwegian railways and refer to our previous proposal for an action plan for the development of crossing tracks. We also believe that a good and reliable rail service, with the confidence of travellers, is essential for getting more traffic across from road to rail. Delays and cancellations in train traffic help to weaken rail in competition with car and aircraft. Increasing errors and defects in infrastructure and materials have also weakened the punctuality, attractiveness and reputation of the train in recent years. There is cause for concern about a development in which the train loses in competition with other modes of transport. I recommend the Committee's position, and come back to several points of view in later articles.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e239	Document 8:89 S for 2009-2010 from four representatives of the Progress Party, Bård Hoksrud, Jan-Henrik Fredriksen, Inbjørg Vokskesen and myself, concerns an expanded and binding national investment plan for the main road network. All other parties reject the proposal on the matter. The decision, which is supported by a majority of the Committee, will require the document to be annexed to the minutes. The parties to the Government have a proposal not to approve the proposal. The representatives of the other parties will explain their views. I use my post to talk about the Progress Party's view on the matter. I refer to the proposals of the Progress Party. Let me first say what the proposal isn't. It is not a rematch on treatment, debate and voting related to the National Transport Plan 2010–2019, as the Stortinget concluded last June. It is a proposal for an expanded and binding investment plan for the main road network for the period 2011 to 2019 with an additional investment framework of 200 billion. kr. It is a proposal to use new investment tools to finance investments of 200 billion. kr. It is a proposal that will ensure far more investment in the way and with clear, long-term and predictable funding. Why this proposal? The Norwegian model of a national transport plan provides a framework that does not meet the need. It is of little benefit to boast of nominal increase in crowning appropriations compared to the year before after the previous plan when the gap between needs and plan content leads to an increase of 70 years before Norway has a modern, efficient, safe and environmentally friendly road network. When the government's proposal for the NTP came, the Federation said that the plan was not ‘sufficient to ensure the country a time-consuming road and rail system. Norway will continue to lose ground in relation to our competitors’ countries’. It was also said that the road development continues with the crawling speed. The Norwegian model of a national transport plan does not provide clear, long-term and predictable funding, on the contrary. The investment of the transport plan is weighted annually against all other good objectives in a one-year government budget that is expenditure-based. The Norwegian model, with its national plan providing money in an annual budgetary battle, creates unexplained, short-term and unpredictable funding in the transport sector. The progress party says, "We can't have it like that." We don't want it that way, and of course we don't have to have it that way either. That is not the way we should be if we want to modernise the road network in Norway – in particular the main road network, which is the basic factor for value creation across the country. More and more people agree with the Progress Party. The last one I registered was Bjørn Sund, referred to in the Evening Post last Saturday, June 12. Health has led the development of Aarhus, several OL facilities and the main airport at the Gardermoen. He says what the Progress Party has long said: the projects must be adopted as a whole, with full full funding from the first day, a separate development company authorised to carry out the entire project and with a clear timeframe. As the Progress Party, Sund believes that the state would like to stand as a builder and owner, but the company must have the authority to carry out the entire project. State control plan works best, says the Progress Party – says development expert Bjørn Sund. Professional competence exists both domestically outside the trade unions and abroad, says the Progress Party – says development expert Bjørn Sund. The Progress Party has made proposals for organisation before, and will be able to do so again – transforming state agencies into state enterprises and building through project companies. However, the main tool for speeding up investment in the road sector is the financing tool. This has been illustrated in the 2010 state budget, the first year budget of the National Transport Plan 2010–2019, where there is a lack of money in the first year of the plan. The Road Traffic Information Council said the following: ‘Time road lift’ means a marginal road improvement.’; The starting point for this proposal is that the NTP system, NTP speed and NTP funding are fundamentally inadequate. We'll do something about that. We will introduce a system that distinguishes between annual expenditure budgets and budgets for investments that will last for 50 to 100 years. The Progress Party will introduce a system in which the Storting will adopt a binding investment plan for 10 years, with binding funding – it provides clear, not unclear, long-term, not short-term, and predictable, not unpredictable, financing of road development in Norway. The Progress Party proposes that such a binding national investment plan for the comprehensive road network for the period 2011 to 2019, with a framework of 200 billion. kr, shall be an addition to NTP and that drafts and proposals for such a plan are submitted in the autumn. It'll give you speed on the road development. We point to two important premises: state regulatory plan and full strain in the first part of the planning period also in order to complete plans. The Progress Party suggests that new funding tools be used. Among the tools Sigbjørn Johnsen recommended, before being bundled with dogmatic socio-economic thinking goods, was the following two: infrastructure funds, which means that parts of the oil revenues are set aside for a fund where the return is used for investment, and debt financing, which means that the state does what is common to all of us – and in all business life – that large investments are paid only in part with cash, the rest are borrowed and paid over time because the benefits and returns last long into the future. I've said it before – and I'm going to have to say it again for a while: No one builds a house in Norway as the state builds roads in Norway. No one builds houses without clear, long-term and predictable financing before the house project starts. When it is started, it is completed as soon as possible. The party of progress is happy that the Right is following us for the most part in the comments. At the same time, we are disappointed that the Right does not fully follow us – to the proposal, which is an order for an investment plan, an order for a case on the use of new tools, with a frame of 200 billion. kr to the main road network. It's a little bit surprising, because we have common notes, which include that. are from both parties, i.e. both from the Party of Advancement and the Right: ‘These members believe that experience from their own and other countries provides a fully sound basis for policy decisions relating to the use of a multiannual development plan with clear and binding multiannual funding based on new funding tools.’ The proposal is not on the right. The difference between the remark and the proposal is two things: that it should be done, and that the financial envelope is set at 200 billion. kr. So as opposed to the Progress Party, the Right will wait. The Christian People's Party has very brief comments, mostly on the environment and at least on the road and half the sentence that the Christian People's Party «is a supporter of increased investment in the tribal road network». Outside the hall I have registered that the head of the Transport Committee from the Christian People's Party is clear that more needs to be granted, but to the invitation to a dedicated investment plan and new financing tools, there is no point. While the roads fall away.

The reason why the Advance Party wants this anchoring is, among other things: the fact that the mineral law adopted in 2009 had been in force for more than 15 years. We cannot continue to do this, but quickly and with quality come up with a strategy that the mining industry can live with, and not only live with, but also give it the opportunity to grow and take care of the resources that exist. These developments have increased demand for all types of consumer goods and increased global demand for raw materials, in particular energy products, metals and minerals. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e1429	Representatives Nesvik, Bredvold and Treldal have proposed that the main agreement be terminated by the State with the agricultural organisations. The majority of the Committee rejects this proposal. The main agreement for agriculture regulates the negotiation of an industrial agreement between the country and the industry. The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the Progress Party, emphasise the value of commitment to cooperation between the State and industry. The main agreement states that there will be consistency between the rights given to the industry and the obligations it assumes. Through the main agreement, the industry also assumes responsibility for implementing the agricultural agreement, which is very important for the practical implementation of an important agreement. The majority point out that agriculture is a resource-based industry, and that the natural resources for farming vary greatly in our long-term country. Therefore, the market mechanism alone cannot take account of the agricultural and social interests that the Stortinget has established in different contexts. The contract work shall not only take care of the income policy and the relationship in the industry. Agriculture creates important societal values such as food security, culture, environmental quality and living communities. Therefore, it is reasonable that the State is an active party in the relationship and in cooperation with the agricultural organisations. Agriculture is also an important basis for other industries such as the food industry and experience-based tourism and tourism. It is important that we have a network of agreements to ensure stable conditions for industry and for the industries that build on agriculture, while at the same time taking care of important societal interests that create quality of life throughout the country. Economic developments in Norway have been more stable than in any country it is natural to compare with. The Government Party states in its recommendation that tripartite cooperation between the state, workers and employers can be an important cause of the stability we have in the Norwegian economy. And the agricultural agreement is an important part of the whole thing: the Norwegian, or Nordic, model, which provides good stability for quality of life and jobs. The members of the Progress Party want to cancel the main agreement for agriculture and allow agriculture to evolve on the basis of the framework conditions offered by the market and society. The members of the Right do not support the proposal of the Progress Party to cancel the main agreement, but they want to remove some regulations and strengthen competition and reduce transfers to agriculture. I expect that the representatives of the minority parties will argue for their views during the debate here today. SV notes that this case clearly shows the differences in Norwegian policy. The right side of the policy will eliminate solidarity and replace it with market management. In this case, it will lead to extensive deportation from the districts. This, in turn, will lead to a reduction in the use of important national resources such as pastures and roughage production. Deregulation will make it more profitable to stop as a farmer and less profitable to continue in agriculture. There will be a strong depopulation of the countryside around the country. The right-hand side indicates that we also have today's dislocation and centralisation. It's true that we've had a decline in the number of uses over time, but we're now seeing the contours of stabilizing that development. And the solution is not to give up the fight against centralization and depopulation from the countryside, the solution is to use tools that can create living settlements in the future. There is little doubt that the deregulation and market management of agriculture will lead to dramatic depopulation and concentration of agricultural activity. The progress batch should confirm that this is the price they are willing to pay to reduce government transfers and perhaps bring down food prices. The party of progress should be honest enough to admit that their policies will lead to a dramatic deportation, a dramatic reduction in the use of natural resources such as roughage and pasturage.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e1483	The party regards self-employed farmers as a support bar for the creation of value and nutrition in many places in the country. We want the farmer to have development opportunities in the future. A robust agricultural industry contributes to achieving important objectives of continued significant Norwegian food production, differentiated housing and a beautiful cultural landscape. Agriculture, like, for example: the tourism industry and the fishing industry are of great importance in ensuring positive development in many communities. We record that the agricultural conversion continues. It is estimated that 35,000 farms with housing are empty, many of them due. Conversion to larger and more efficient units is necessary to ensure that the agricultural industry becomes more forward-looking and less dependent on transfers. We believe that agricultural policy should facilitate this better. In principle, it is of concern that individual industries negotiate their own framework conditions directly with the State and conclude agreements with major economic consequences outside the ordinary budgetary procedure, and that the annual decisions on the framework conditions of agriculture are not a pay bill but a determination of the possibilities for generating income. The significant deviations between the promised income development in agriculture in the individual settlements and the achieved development show that the basis of calculation used for the settlements is thin. The Progress Party wants farmers, as other traders, to be responsible for their own value creation according to the framework conditions set by society, and believes it is important to give agriculture new opportunities to develop and reduce dependence on public support. For the settlement to be seen in conjunction with other policy priorities, it is clear that the settlement is dealt with in the context of the State budget and follows the calendar year. The State shall continue to grant financial support to agriculture, but such grants must be more targeted and less bureaucratic than they are today. The Progress Party considers it central to strengthen the farmer's ownership rights for his own use, for example. to propose the removal of a dwelling obligation. Occupancy is a barrier to the future development of agriculture and forestry in Norway, and it is not proven to contribute to settlement. In many cases, price regulation provides artificially low prices, prevents turnover and increases the risk of investments on the farm. A high distance between the market price and the regulated price also provides a basis for cases where parts of the purchase fee are hidden from the authorities. We believe that there is a serious interference in ownership, and that the state forces people who want to sell the farm to take a lower price than the buyer is willing to pay. The Party of Progress is strongly critical of the agricultural policy pursued by the Red-Green Government and cannot therefore in any way support the negotiated agricultural agreement. The new agreement does not provide for a new direction in agricultural policy, despite the fact that there is a great need for a change of course. Despite the fact that the red-green government announced a strong change in agricultural policy when it came to power, it has not happened. This year, too, there was a big gap between the requirement submitted by the farmers' organisations and the offer that came from the state. The negotiated agreement also amounts to approx. half of the initial requirement of the organisations. It is necessary to establish a normal market-based system for agricultural products managed by supply and demand. In the case of under-coverage in the market, the producer will be able to take a higher price, while the consumer can choose other products if the price of the product is too high. In that case, prices of products will vary in line with the market, and producers must be market-oriented in their production. In a situation that today, where consumers demand more than Norwegian farmers can produce, a market-based system will contribute to higher production. In a market-based system, competition between manufacturers will also arise, leading to better products, innovation and more cost-effective production. This will contribute to better income opportunities for those producers who are skilled, innovative and good businessmen. The Party will point out that market-based systems operate in all other markets and that there is therefore no reason to exempt agriculture from this. The sharp decline in the number of farmers and use will continue in the coming years, probably with increased force. Agricultural actors have far too little confidence in the future, and the confidence in the government's agricultural policy is at a low level. The policy that has been against agriculture for many years is a socialist experiment that has contributed to the Norwegian farmer being caught up in a ‘net’ and a system from which it is almost impossible to escape. Agricultural policy is characterised by a strong drive to regulate industry down to the smallest detail, and public bureaucrats and a very detailed regulatory framework have taken over the direction of agricultural development. The management of individual farmers over their own property and employment has been lost, and farmers are now more to be regarded as public employees and practitioners of a public policy than self-employed workers in the food production industry, as they should have been initially. This has led to a lack of creativity, ability to invest and a desire to take risks in large parts of agriculture. I find it strange that both the agricultural organisations and the Central Party support such a policy that is constantly leading to fewer farmers and great dissatisfaction with the income situation and opportunities among industry actors. It is therefore important to point out that the agricultural agreement has become so complex and complicated that, apart from the central bureaucracy of the Ministry of Agriculture and Food, it is possible to get involved in all aspects of the system and fully understand it. Nor have any other OECD countries found this appropriate. This may be harmful to other export industries, including: the export of fish, when a milk toll is placed on a fading 388 pst. Norway has to contend that there will be a reduction in the protection of imports in the wake of international developments through WTO negotiations. The development offers a gradual increase in competition for Norwegian agricultural products. Despite a high level of customs protection, the Norwegian authorities have not set out a plan for meeting such a trend in Norwegian agriculture. This is very critical. The result may be that Norwegian agriculture will have a blue day when the outcome of the WTO negotiations becomes known. We need a new development. Robust production based on market economy criteria must be top priority and must be developed before import protection falls. We see today that the Norwegian food industry is under pressure from abroad, and it is important that Norwegian agricultural policy ensures that the industry receives raw materials at a competitive price. In order to achieve this, it is necessary to structuralise agriculture so as to reduce unit costs. While industry is competitive vis-à-vis foreign operators in terms of processing and industrial production, raw materials costs are too high in relation to our neighbouring countries and may threaten important jobs and value creation in industry by increasing imports. The food industry accounts for about 20 pst. of employment and value added in industry. A common feature is that production is constantly labour intensive, despite the use of very modern technologies by enterprises. Norway must therefore use the remaining time to adapt the primary production level to the new everyday life that everyone sees coming in a relatively near future. It is therefore important that the government has an active attitude to this, and not that today is entirely passive to the gradual development. The outcome of such a policy would seriously harm the food industry. The Progress Party is disappointed that the government is not taking a settlement with the agricultural policy management system and the legal basis of agricultural policy, which was largely designed during the mid-war period under completely different conditions. We record that the result of more than 70 years of a controlled agricultural industry proves that the policy pursued has failed, because it has not achieved the objectives of housing, employment and income levels. The number of operational uses and the number of active farmers are falling rapidly, and it is expected that the same take-off will continue with the agricultural agreement of the year. This is also stated by Norwegian Farmers and Small Farmers as they broke out of farming. New figures for rural settlement development show a strongly declining trend. This must be a major defeat for the government, which has announced a new course in agricultural policy. Moreover, agricultural policy has resulted in significant costs in terms of public expenditure, high food prices and reduced nutrition for farmers. It is therefore important to point out that all restrictions and obstacles imposed by concession restrictions, target prices, production quotas and selective aid schemes have been devastating to farmers. The promotion party will therefore give a strong priority to a reorientation where deregulation, red tape and competition are put at the top of the agenda. The process must be initiated as soon as possible in order to avoid a future risk of a complete industry. We have great faith in Norwegian agriculture and in Norwegian farmers. The majority in Stortinget has shown a distrust of the Norwegian peasantry by severely restricting the freedom of the industry. I am not least in doubt that Norwegian agriculture has great opportunities to develop into a competitive industry that can also compete on an international market with special products. Norwegian climate conditions, genetic resources, ecology and farm competence are specific advantages that can be exploited for our benefit in an international market. Payment willingness consumers in the EU and in other markets are looking for safe and healthy foods, which are also of high quality and taste. By releasing the farmer, farmers will have all the prerequisites to deliver such products here in the country, but it requires targeted investment and cultivation of a entrepreneurial culture, as experienced by other industries. Norwegian industry and many other industries have become internationally leading within their segments. This has meant that Norway has a high level of competence in internationalisation, marketing, product development, quality assurance and other elements that are important for international success. I am addressing the proposals made by the Progress Party, either alone or together with the Right. I would also like to inform you that we are going to support the Left's proposal No. 23 and that we will support the Left's proposal No. 26 subsidized.

Subsidies and import barriers in developed countries prevent developing countries from producing food on their own market and export their products to developed countries. WTO negotiations and a possible global food crisis, with food shortages and significant increases in prices on the world market, call for a more cohesive agricultural policy. The left wants Norway to go ahead and continue to reduce import barriers and production-related subsidies. I ask the representative from SV, who was concerned about this, to hear what the left said here. In such a perspective, the agricultural agreement of the year is several steps in the wrong direction. On the one hand, the protection of Norwegian milk products is increased and, on the other hand, the system of export aid for Norwegian cheese production is maintained and continued. This is, in the view of the Left, a double standard, and there is little reason why the Minister of Agriculture has repeatedly warned that this system should be abolished, and that the Minister of Foreign Affairs, as late as in Stortinget on 26 May of this year, categorically stated that he intended that the export aid ‘should be phased out’. I therefore refer to a distributed proposal from the Left that the Stortinget begs the government to wind up the system of export aid for cheese production through the price equalisation system. There needs to be more diversity in agriculture. Local authorities must be given greater freedom to see agricultural policy in the context of other industrial policies. The Left therefore wants a full review of the legislation, with a view to a strong modernisation, fewer forms, simpler guidelines and gradually reduced market regulation. The left wants to continue the agricultural negotiations but as a pure income agreement between farmers and countries. All other grants and schemes which do not have a direct effect on the income of the farmer should be transferred to ordinary government budget items. Investing in local food and drink production will be crucial for future agriculture, tourism and not least for living and active rural communities throughout the country. Legislation relating to production, distribution and serving is often a hindrance to the success of producers. The left will stimulate a reindeer for Norwegian food culture, including: by increasing the focus on geographical origin marking and the liberalisation of the legislation for the processing of food and drink on its own farm, without prejudice to health safety. In this way, I refer to the proposal from the Left, which the Health and Welfare Committee has to deal with, to allow demonstration sales and serving on visits to production premises or in connection with them, and to allow producers of alcoholic beverages to inform about their products on their own Internet page, as the Vinmonopolet currently has access to. I have registered that both the Minister of Agriculture and the Minister of Economic Affairs have expressed their support for the proposal, and this time expect the representatives of the Government Parties to follow up. Norwegian agriculture has an important role to play in working for a more sustainable society. Agriculture produces food in a low level of environmental impact in a global context. It takes care of the cultural landscape and produces renewable materials and energy. The main environmental challenges in agriculture are to reduce greenhouse gas emissions of methane and natural gas, to increase organic production and to increase the production of bioenergy, without jeopardising biodiversity. I note that there is a reasonable distance between the previous Minister of Agriculture Terje Riis-Johansen’s statements that ‘every abandoned farm is a defeat’ to today’s end, where the government parties write that it is important to focus on the issues in order to prevent these developments – i.e. the structuralisation and closure of use – from going too far. It is good that the brutal truth of reality has opened its eyes to the industrial politicians of the government party. What's less good is the promises the voters have made, where it was suggested that you should actually reverse this development. Finally, I take up the proposals from the Left which have been distributed in the Chamber and at the same time warn that the Left will support the proposals. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e1621	Norwegian agricultural policy is characterised by stability for farmers, for the food industry and for Norwegian consumers. Stability and predictability of framework conditions are a good thing that traders place a strong emphasis on the authorities. It gives greater certainty about right decisions, it gives less risk. Looking at the world around us, it has been marked by great instability in recent years, initiated by the food crisis, the commodity crisis, the financial crisis, and not least the impacts of weather and climate. We are somewhat protected from this reality in Norway, but we are not unaffected. The Norwegian food sector is also exposed to competition, and it is necessary to take into account policy-making. There are, of course, limitations in the scope of action, but the result for Norwegian actors is clearly more predictable framework conditions than for farmers and industry in other countries. Important elements of the framework conditions for the farmers are defined in the formalised cooperation process of the agricultural agreement. It gives agriculture rights and obligations, and I believe it is a natural part of the Norwegian interaction model. We get better solutions over time through accountability and cooperation and the mutual commitment that comes with it. I am therefore pleased that there is so much support to continue the negotiating institute in this Chamber, as it appears in the vote on document 8:118 S, with proposals for termination of the main agreement. As I read this proposal for a representative, the rapporteurs primarily disagree with the agricultural policy that is being pursued. But agricultural negotiations are conducted within the framework laid down by the Storting. On that basis, the agricultural agreement will continue to be an appropriate tool for the further development of the interaction between the public and the farmers, which carries out an important work for the Norwegian community. Many of the social tasks of agriculture are the production of collective goods, for which by definition there are no markets. It is reasonable, in my opinion, that the farmers have the right to negotiate with society the terms of this production. Under the main agreement, agriculture assumes responsibility for implementing measures in accordance with the agricultural agreement. Agriculture has assumed financial responsibility for overproduction through the contractual system. It provides incentives for industry to maintain a long-term balance in commodity markets. It provides effective regulation, and I have to say that I am surprised when those in this room are deeply concerned about reducing bureaucracy, at the same time go in to conclude the main agreement. State regulation would provide significantly greater red tape and higher regulatory public costs. I note that the Right wants an interim solution that maintains the negotiations, but with a periodicisation that follows the ordinary fiscal year. It is set to be a first step on the road to major structural changes, reduced transfers and less red tape. It is a little unclear what practical consequences this is supposed to have for the negotiations themselves. The agricultural agreement already follows the calendar year for the budget and is presented in the Ministry of Agriculture and Food's budget proposal. When negotiations take place on spring, it is in order to ensure long-term and predictability, including: by clarifying target prices and other provisions prior to the forthcoming production season, as is the case in some other countries. The agricultural agreement currently being discussed by the Stortinget is a clear agricultural agreement. It is clear in the priorities of the use of tools in relation to policy objectives, on the basis of the challenges we are facing. Trends in industry revenue have been too weak over too long. New generations of farmers also require better welfare arrangements, because over time we cannot have clearly worse agricultural conditions than in society else. And we see that production and land development are significantly weaker in some areas where the grass-based livestock population is dominant than it is in stronger agricultural areas. This agreement is a clear follow-up to last year's agricultural agreements and contributes over time to the necessary prioritisation of agricultural and food industries. The agreement facilitates an increase in income of 5 p.m., or approximately 11 500 kroner per annual work in 2011, with the assumptions underlying the agreement made by the parties. This means that from the beginning of the previous government period, an increase in income has been provided that is slightly higher than on average for other groups, provided that the same estimates of cost developments are made. The welfare systems are strengthened by 5 p.m., and the demands of agriculture also pointed out that welfare systems are clearly strengthened during this government. The agreement also contains several measures which were assessed as a whole to strengthen the grass-based livestock and milk production separately. However, grass-based livestock farming is said to be such a large proportion of Norwegian agriculture as to be a demanding priority. With an increase of 375 million in appropriations. kr the premium rates for the grass-based livestock population are increased by approximately 490 million. kr. It is possible by targeting the means and disposal of savings. It is in my opinion to give priority. The operating grant rates for milk and beef are increasing by 140 million. kr. It has a strong structure profile. At the same time, subsidies are increased for the first units of livestock subsidies, while the area limit for premium for roughage is moved from 200 to 250 dechar. These grips must be seen in the context. It strengthens the compensation for scallopes, which are particularly linked to the building stock, while at the same time reducing the possibility of unwanted adjustment of grants with the areas that can be moved between active enterprises through rental. One important element in the settlement was that the government went in and said that the transition to the percentage of liquid milk and cream could be used as a basis for the agreement. It provides greater protection and security on the domestic market. And that was a natural part of the priority of milk production. There are areas with more difficult production conditions and weaker development than other areas that are most important to achieve with the policy instruments we have. It is these areas that will suffer if we leave the market alone. This is why these areas are also given priority this year. The freight systems are strongly reinforced, which makes a significant contribution to equal profitability regardless of geography. Freight schemes are a targeted instrument in the centre–periphery dimension, and this is important in agricultural policy. It strengthens the competitiveness of edge producers – it becomes more economically interesting for all reception facilities to buy their production. This will also strengthen the basis for continuing agriculture — and keeping the land in operation — throughout the country. Improvements in cargo arrangements, together with reduced price differences in power feed, also contributed to the rightness of reducing some district subsidies. The aim of the district grants is to contribute to providing equivalent income opportunities in order to maintain a geographical distribution of production. The funds must then be used where necessary, not in the areas where market shares are held. Therefore, it was also appropriate to differentiate the subsidies to the pig population and to differentiate the operating grant for milk production. All areas receive a higher grant, but the increase is at least on the Iron. The operating grant is particularly important for the smallest enterprises and this amendment provides for a greater increase in areas where production conditions are more difficult and the economies of scale are smaller. The agreement also concludes the harmonisation of the operating grant for milk production between types of enterprises that started in 2004. This means that the grant measure does not discriminate between different types of milk production companies of the same size. Equally large undertakings are granted equivalent operating grants, regardless of whether any of the entity's allowances have been purchased, leased or several allowance holders have merged into a joint venture. Thus, cooperatives established after 1 July 2004 are also treated in a non-discriminatory manner with those established before that date. As a result, local conditions determine the choice of organisational form rather than the adjustment of grants. In the setting, the representatives of the Progress Party and the Right say that ‘conversion to larger and more efficient units is necessary’. At the same time, it is pointed out that agricultural policy has failed because the number of active agricultural holdings is still declining. Does the Progress Party and the Right really believe that the structural changes in agriculture would have been less affected by the deregulation and reduction of transfers they are in favour of? I believe that a shift to larger and more efficient units must mean an increased take-off of businesses. The Progress Party also continues to reiterate the need for food producers to have more control over their own production and for politicians and bureaucrats to have less influence. Does the Progress Party really believe that farmers are more able to control their own production in a deregulated market, where the primary stage is weakened and each farmer becomes a downstream contract producer? I think it's going to be the opposite. Through the settlement, we have continued an income promise for the farmers. And we've been giving very clear priority to the instruments. That is what policy is supposed to do. My ambition is to continue to strengthen agriculture and pursue the goals we have set.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e1829	The main agreement regulates formal and mutually committed cooperation between the country and the agricultural economic operators on the implementation of the agricultural policy defined by the Stortinget. The agreement regulates the process, the parties' rights and obligations, but not the material content of the contract itself, which may seem to some here believe. A reason why it is useful to cancel the main agreement is that it is not right for an industry to negotiate directly with the state. They want the market to control everything. When it comes to controlling everything on the market, the Labour Party is fundamentally indistinguishable from the Progress Party. We believe that the market is a good server, but a bad master. The market must be controlled in this way. Lack of market governance was a main reason for the financial crisis, and that is what the Progress Party applauds. Another factor for the market to control everything is that the social tasks imposed on agriculture are not implemented and followed up if it is the market on its own. This can be good as society demands, but there's no market for it there and there. Nor does the market take into account Community values or, for example: climate challenges. The agreement has proved to be able to handle the changes in externally given framework conditions. The content of the annual agreements is also developed within the framework of the negotiations. This is evident by the fact that in recent years a number of adjustments have been made to the target price system, market regulation mechanisms and budgetary support – as an adaptation to international rules. In Soria Moria II we say red-green that we will continue the agricultural negotiations. I'm glad of this. Agriculture agreements are simply a tool for implementing the agricultural policy agreed by the Stortinget, a tool for the Advance Party to remove. The contractual system can also be seen as a natural part of the Norwegian model of compulsory cooperation between the public and the labour and business sectors. The Norwegian model is based on tripartite cooperation. Fortunately, Norway has a high level of organisation and a wide representation of employees in companies. This is a strength. Tripartite cooperation has led to economic stability, smaller conflicts and a common social responsibility. The model is the hallmark of mutual respect and cooperation. As the Progress Party is also opposed to trade unions, and thus the Norwegian model of tripartite cooperation, I do not take my mouth to heart when I claim that economic stability is a lack of success with the Progress Party's policy. The level of conflict will increase, and it will be up to the individual and the market to take social responsibility. And as I said, the market doesn't care about the community. In my view, it is of great value that agricultural policy is designed in close cooperation with industry itself. This cooperation is precisely stimulating co-responsibility and active involvement in the implementation of agricultural policy. So there's another moment I want to mention. It's not like we're sitting here in Oslo, we always have the full overview of what an industry needs. Every shoe prints, knows the best that wears the shoe. I don't know how many councils the Progress Party has with farmers, I just have to admit that – nor any meetings with the Small User Team and the Norwegian Bond Team – but I've had a relatively large number of such meetings over the years with organizations and with individual peasants. And the cry to release, to this day, I have not heard – not once. The Western farmer in Sogn and Fjordane, whom I know best, is the hallmark of driving in foreign terrain and small units. I have not met a farmer from home who thinks that a deregulated market, where the primary link becomes weak and each farmer should be a contact producer for the later stages, will be a strengthening of the farmer and the agricultural industry – on the contrary. If all operations must be market-based, e.g. Sogn and Fjordane-bonds will meet faintly. Here is little possibility to drive big. We red-green have announced that we want a new agricultural message. This I look forward to working with. Together with the industry and organisations, we are going to chisel out a healthy, good agricultural policy and a living community in the whole country, for the benefit of consumers as well.

So far in the debate, no one has defended this context. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e2075	The paradox of higher labour productivity and declining real incomes for agriculture has been described by the English economist Hans Singer with regard to the situation in developing countries. As a result of perfect competition on the commodity market internationally, commodity producers have to surrender the productivity gains to customers. Imperfect competition in industry and parts of the service sector, such as banks and food chains, leads them to a far greater degree to retain the benefits of their own productivity development. The crisis in the 1930s struck first in agriculture, with disastrously falling prices. To remedy this, all countries gave market power to farmers’ organisations. That is why all rich countries also protect their agricultural production. The first World War II farm survey reports understood these economic mechanisms and thus understood that agriculture cannot only increase capital spending and thereby increase labour productivity in order to make its economic problems more effective. Agriculture meets decreasing yields as production increases. This means that the more intensive production, the less yields, agriculture has as an industry for each new unit the input factor in production. This is particularly important for us in Norway for the use of power feed in grass-based livestock. When Bill Gates makes a device of Windows Vista, it might cost him $500 million, while additional devices that are distributed online may just cost 1 to 2 cents to produce. These are economies of scale which characterise industry and the provision of advanced services, which are the opposite of the situation of farmers. This fundamental fact is important to discuss in order to find a new set of instruments for developing Norwegian agriculture in line with international requirements and national societal tasks. Fair payment for social work must be a red-green policy plate. From a historical and an international perspective, the largest strength of the Norwegian agricultural model is the owner-occupier working on his own farm, either on a full-time or part-time basis. We do not have the tradition of farming as an industry in companies with limited financial responsibilities, such as limited liability companies. A company's agriculture requires the usual return on capital employed and income in line with the wage or salary of comparable occupations. For example, if the income in the industry is not sufficient to ensure this, it must: the holder of the farm is working more or less diligently for the company. Most people will eventually understand that this is not sustainable, rather a permanent form of operation. It is therefore necessary to put an end to any agricultural policy favouring company agriculture. Whether this company's agriculture has to compete on the same terms as the Norwegian agricultural model with family farming full-time or part-time, developments will quickly show that family ownership is socially sovereign and business best for domestic and soil workers.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e2090	The debate shows, not surprisingly, well-known lines of agricultural policy. The Christian People's Party has been and is committed to giving farmers the opportunity for reasonable income development, because it is vital not least to ensure the recruitment of the profession. The moment of truth for agricultural policy is in many ways when the next generation is at the time of the elections and is it going to be considered – is it going to take over what the parents have built up, or is it going to be a different path? That choice is kind of a test of the quality of the agricultural policy we have. Very often, there is an investment need for such a choice, for such an acquisition. There may be worn-out operational buildings, there may be expectations of future requirements that the previous generation has not dared to take into account. This will lead to a rise in new investments, new operating buildings, etc., for the next generation to bet. Thus, there are also expectations of reasonable income trends and the conditions for investing that are essential for ensuring the recruitment of the profession. The Christian People's Party has therefore stated in our remarks in the preamble that we will stress that agriculture must be given a framework which will enable the industry to renew the apparatus. In this context, important instruments will be to ensure an agricultural settlement that provides a reasonable level of income. It will also be to review depreciation rates, which will of course be the investment framework under Innovation Norway, but it will also be to a greater extent to take into account that some of these young people who consider whether to continue to invest see co-operation as the only possibility for them to dare to do so. It is in the interest of the new generations to impose other requirements on the working environment, to impose other requirements on leisure time and to feel the need to spread the risk of making the necessary large investments in a forward-looking use. The Christian People's Party is therefore promoting – in line with our view – our policy on fair income development, but we also believe that it is important to have a greater focus on the investment framework to ensure that new generations continue to drive Norwegian agriculture.

Key challenges and tasks: Ensuring, developing and strengthening the reindeer industry poses challenging challenges for both industry and government. I believe, however, that we do not get beyond the very core of the problem – the predatory wildlife populations themselves. In my view, we need to start work as soon as possible to reduce the stocks that are now larger than the agreed stock targets to the agreed targets. And I expect the government to speed up the determination of the new stock targets, as mentioned in the Soria Moria statement. The life-set and culture of the southern families can only be preserved by active reindeer farming. The question is whether Stortinget is willing to take the consequences.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e2872	The Maritime Directorate moved out of Oslo to Haugesund in 2006. This led to the loss of very many competent staff. Almost 84 pst. by the staff of the Directorate for Maritime Affairs closed during the transfer of the Directorate. In such a situation, it is essential that an ability to ensure that such loss of competence does not have an impact on the quality of the proceedings. The Ministry has stressed that many new employees in the Maritime Directorate work with good expertise. However, the National Audit Office’s audit revealed a number of weaknesses in its revision of the Maritime Directorate’s document control of ships and seafarers. The findings were so serious that the Riksrevision stopped working on a full administrative audit and reported on the findings we are dealing with today in Stortinget, in order to ensure that improvement could be made rapidly. It is an assessment agreed by the Committee. The study shows that legislation in some areas is unclear. Despite this, the Maritime Directorate has failed to incorporate procedures to ensure the highest possible level of regulatory practice. There are limited written procedural procedures and procedures to correct procedural errors are missing. The Directorate has a small overview of the number of cases and lacks an overview of the length of proceedings. Furthermore, it has been revealed that pending decisions are deliberately withdrawn from the public records to give the company the decision on which they are based, time to turn around. This is a form of administrative practice which, of course, is not acceptable in 2010 and which the Committee considers to be a serious breach of good governance. Furthermore, there is reason to stress the importance of good administrative and documentation procedures for maritime safety. The Committee considers that good practice practices are important in order to ensure maritime safety. Without speaking on behalf of the entire committee, I allow myself to point out that I think Minister of Industry Trond Giske underestimates the potential damage in the breach of good administration and the lack of internal control revealed in this area. However, on behalf of the Committee, I can say that we have noticed that the Ministry of Industry and Trade in the letter of allocation for the current financial year itself has underlined the importance of the Directorate setting up a satisfactory system to ensure good and systematic governance information. Although some of the facts mentioned in the Riksrevision report have been addressed in the 2010 letter of award, the Committee is pleased that the Riksrevision will follow up these findings within three years. It remains to be said, then, that the Committee is unanimous in this matter on all points.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e2893	The overall objective of the Directorate for Maritime Affairs is to be a visible and clear operator of maritime safety. The Maritime Directorate shall contribute to the compliance of sailing ships with the applicable legislation. For the shipping nation Norway, this overall objective is of the utmost importance. Norway, as one of the leading nations in maritime activities, may not be familiar with any ambiguity in this area. Safety at sea is of very great social and economic importance to our nation. Therefore, it is important that the focus is on the optimum functioning of the tools that we as a nation have in this context and thus creates security for all parties. In the present investigation, the National Audit Office has revealed procedural weaknesses in important parts of the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs’ activities. The Court of Auditors notes that procedures and checklists have been developed to a small extent in the proceedings and that decisions have been taken that are not in line with the legislation. It also mentions aspects such as the non-journal nature of important information and the fact that decisions have been made on a delayed basis without the Maritime Directorate having issued a conversion decision. These are aspects on which all parties are engaged to have the highest standard. Some kind of doubt in such areas is no good. It is argued that when the Maritime Directorate moved from Oslo to Haugesund, the Directorate lost 84 pst. by the crew, and that this occurred at the same time as there was a large growth in the industry. Everyone understands that this was a major challenge for the directorate, and that it resulted in a huge labour pressure for all employees – both those who joined Haugesund, and those who were newly added. Let me add that I do not regard the move as a negative for the future recruitment and operation of the Maritime Directorate. The Directorate is now in place in an area where maritime competence is strong, which is undoubtedly positive for the future recruitment of the best professionals. I understand that this is already a reality, and I am absolutely sure that this will be even better in the time to come. Here I want to draw a parallel to when the Polar Institute moved from Oslo to Tromsø. Then there were many employees who jumped off, and the predictions were gloomy, but today the Polar Institute has a completely unique competence in its field. I think this is precisely because of the location. I have noticed that the Department takes the situation in the Maritime Directorate very seriously, that additional appropriations were granted in 2008 to deal with the challenges of crewing and competence, and that this has been continued later. I therefore take a positive view of the situation, and I feel confident that all parties are doing what is possible to remedy the matters raised by the National Audit Office. In particular, it is important for the future recruitment and for the directorate's staff to focus on all the tasks associated with this important directorate.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e2921	The National Audit Office’s report is an important input to the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs’ efforts to continuously improve the process. A number of measures have already been taken to improve the quality of the management of the Directorate. The aim of the National Audit Office’s investigation has been to examine the extent to which the Maritime Directorate has the necessary procedures and systems to ensure that the proceedings are carried out in accordance with good practice. The Ministry’s management of the Maritime Directorate is based on an overall approach to the entire internal control system, including risk management, control activity, information and communication, and follow-up/revision of the directorate’s internal management systems. This is reflected in the Ministry’s letter of assignment to the Directorate, where it is clear that risk management should be an integral part of the etat management dialogue. The Maritime Directorate has some challenges. This is mentioned in the Ministry's 2010 letter of award. One of the main objectives of the Maritime Directorate is quality at all stages. It also provides for a continuous improvement in the management of the directorate. The Maritime Directorate consists of a number of competent staff working on a very complex subject area. It is important to point out that the National Audit Office has examined a limited number of cases and units in the Directorate. The Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs handles about 130 000 documents annually. In recent years, however, the Maritime Directorate has been in a special situation. Some of the background to the challenges of the Directorate may be linked to the fact that the move to Haugesund coincided with a strong growth period in the industry. While the directorate experienced loss of competence, it was difficult to recruit staff with maritime and technical competence. For periods, the directorate has had many undeclared positions. Today, however, the Maritime Directorate is fully staffed, both at its headquarters and at the stations along the coast. The Government briefed the Government of the revised State Budget for 2008 on major new construction activities in the shipping industry, resulting in additional work and increased expenditure of the Maritime Directorate for Supervision, Control and Approval. Based on information provided by the Directorate for Maritime Affairs, the Ministry of Industry and Trade considered that the personnel situation in 2008 did not represent an immediate safety risk, but that measures should be taken to prevent the long-term reduction of safety at sea. In order to ensure sufficient resources and professional competence for the implementation of defined objectives in key areas of maritime safety and environment, the operational appropriations of the Directorate were increased by 28 million. kr. The operational budget of the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs continued to be strengthened in 2009 and 2010. This has contributed to increased recruitment and a better balance between tasks and resources. In 2009, the Government carried out an evaluation of the relocation of seven State supervision units from Oslo, including the Maritime Directorate. The report shows, among other things: that the staff of the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs have higher formal competence, but lower real competence, after the transfer, and that this indicates vulnerability for a few more years. The Ministry has highlighted in its 2010 letter of award the need for the Directorate to focus on further training of its staff. The Directorate for Maritime Affairs has worked for a long time to develop procedures and procedures to ensure good quality of the procedure. However, the study of the Riksrevision shows that some work remains to be done to complete and implement the new procedures. The Directorate for Maritime Affairs is responsible for a comprehensive regulatory framework. Our national legislation is largely based on international legislation negotiated by the UN International Maritime Organisation (IMO). The Directorate has started work on possible simplifications of this legislation. I also point out that the IMO carried out an audit of Norway as a flag and port State in 2007. The audit revealed no deviations from the Directorate’s implementation of international legislation. Norway is a member of the Port State Control regime of the Paris Memorandum of Understanding on Port State Control. The objective is for each port State to inspect 25 pst annually. of the total number of foreign vessels. Checks are directed at all ships calling at the ports of the Parties, irrespective of the flag State to which they belong. Based on the checks carried out, the Paris MOU prepares a white, grey or blacklist classifying the standard ships. Norway is on the White List, which shows that there are few detentions of Norwegian ships as part of port State control in other countries. If the Maritime Directorate had not adopted safety standards as part of its certification of Norwegian ships, several ships would be detained abroad. ESA, the EFTA Surveillance Authority, at EMSA, the European Maritime Safety Agency, carried out audits of the Port State Control of the Directorate of Maritime Administration in 2009. No significant deficiencies were identified in this audit. I recognise that the Maritime Directorate has a clear potential for improvement in the quality of the procedure, which is also underlined in this debate. The Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade, in its 2010 letter of allocation, underlined the importance of the Directorate-General for Maritime Affairs having a well-functioning internal control system. In conclusion, I stress that the department has a good and close governance dialogue with the directorate.

The company will therefore be audited by two different bodies.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3243	As the rapporteur said in his statement, private institutions cover about 60 p.m. of the total capacity of multidisciplinary specialised treatment through agreements with regional health establishments. This shows that private institutions have a very central place in the overall supply of drug addicts. The National Audit Board has assessed whether the Ministry of Health and Welfare and the regional health undertakings have established appropriate systems for the follow-up of the offer of the private institutions to multidisciplinary specialised treatment, hereinafter referred to as ‘TSB’. In the report, they have mentioned seven areas on which they have found the basis to comment. The Russfield is largely based on professional traditions outside the specialist health service, as the Riksrevision itself mentions. The field became part of the specialist health service only at the Rusreform in 2004. There is reason to believe that it will take time to integrate a new field of expertise into the large system of specialist health services. The National Audit Office refers to seven areas to be mentioned here: the TSB was the first specialist health service area where a priority counsellor was established. It happened through the project Correct Priority under the direction of the Health Directorate. In January of this year, the Directorate provided national guidelines for LAR. Corresponding guidance will be provided for all parts of the TSB in four years. These will also include emergency and emergency services for drug addicts. As TSB first became part of the specialist healthcare service in 2004, this field has not been previously reported. From 2009 onwards, all institutions within the TSO will report activity data to the Norwegian patient registry, NPR. These figures will be published in a separate NPR report during the summer. The data will be available as management information in 2011. With effect from 1 September, all relevant health professional units and all private service providers within the TSB will be subject to procedural coding requirements. In this way, we get information about the treatment given to patients in the TSB, this as a basis for further developing the service. Regional health enterprises have a slightly different practice in this area. Health Mid-Norway has its own health business in the drug field and therefore has closer follow-up than the other health businesses. But all health regions have organised cooperation with private institutions. In the work on the Cooperation Reform, private institutions participated in a separate group dealing with patient trajectories. The contributions from private institutions were very useful. Of course, the operation of all private institutions shall be in line with the applicable legislation and regulatory requirements. This is a clear requirement in the procurement process carried out by regional health businesses. The study points out that some institutions have failed to provide adequate internal control systems. This will be followed up appropriately with regional health enterprises. All patients who need long-term and coordinated services have the right to develop an individual plan. The National Audit Board points out that a large proportion of patients in the TSB do not have such a plan. This is going to be better. Regional health establishments have been informed that the proportion given an individual level will be increased. One of three institutions does not have good enough written procedures for interaction with municipal services. Regional health enterprises shall monitor and further develop cooperation between the specialist health services and the municipalities. This still applies. Additional letters shall soon be sent to the 2010 mission document. It is assumed that health firms are familiar with and follow up on the issues addressed by the Riksrevision in their report.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3360	Let me first thank the Committee for a clean and good process in dealing with this matter. I would also like to thank my alternate, Morten Stordalen, who stepped in and took part of the case report while I was on leave. I am very pleased that the committee seems to agree on the importance of the cancer effort in the years to come. A real boost in cancer research and treatment requires that health policy-makers, professionals in treatment and research, as well as the commercial actors in medicine and research, are united. This is not just the challenge of our society, but the major medical challenge of the entire world society. In this field, therefore, there is nothing to prevent small Norway from being a spearhead in the world’s measuring stick. I actually don't think we should have lower ambitions than that. Status may not always be as good as we would have liked. For this reason, I will be allowed to refer to the director of the Health Inspectorate, Lars E. Hanssen, who has gone hard in today's business this month. He punished the management of Norwegian hospitals for poor organisation of cancer treatment. A risk report carried out by supervision states that the risk is too high and that the potential for improvement is ‘significant’ — nothing less. That is why it is commendable that the Right has promoted this proposal. I, the undersigned and the Party of Progress, have earlier in this session also raised an interpellation in the same field. Cancer is a terrible disease that hits, or is going to hit, every third Northman. It not only hits the body physically but is also a major challenge mentally – both for the one who gets the difficult message, and not least the relatives. Getting such a message is heavy and difficult, and those of us who have not received such a message can probably not imagine what it is like. That's exactly why we need the best system, to take care of patients and not least the relatives. It is important to continue the fight against cancer, and it must not be at the country’s legislative assembly to fight against cancer. That's why this case is so important, not just to help today's cancer patients and their relatives out of their disease image, but at least as much to ensure that tomorrow's cancer treatment is both better, more comprehensive – and provides better results than today's medicine can give us. For an overall health and care committee, it is important to point out that the proposals in this matter do not only need to be annexed to the Protocol and then added to the Minister of Health's desk, but that this must, on the contrary, be the beginning of a sustained fight against one of the world's most merciless diseases. Let me get into the recent priority debate, or the prize of human life, if you want. The health director's report that cancer patients cannot count on the very best treatment in the future has alarmed many, including me. The debate is important and correct, but has the wrong focus. You can, and should, of course, ask some questions at the Norwegian Health Service. And you can, and you should, ask questions about the Norwegian health system is too expensive. Yeah, maybe it is, but probably not because of the few expensive patients or because of cancer research. No, rather, I think it is due to poor organisation, overbureaucratisation and, of course, to being a long-term country, which means that we cannot be compared to some of the countries we are compared to. In that priority debate, it is important to ask whether we need to give priority to cancer treatment. My answer and that of the Advancement Party is: Yes, we must. It's at the moment the disease that affects most, and here there's also a great potential for the future to save people, and not only that, but get them back to society in a real way. Then we also have to remember that we are the world leader in the field from before, and we have to take care of the competences we have, and not least develop it. I have received many despaired telephones and e-mails recently after the priority debate started. And I think we'll think a little bit about what this does to people who've got the hard message and are ready to take their life's toughest fight to survive and come back to society. So while deciding what to do and trying to get strength for it, you can read in the newspapers that politicians, and not least the academy, are discussing whether it's worth spending money on this. It must be perceived as a blow to the face of many sick people. There are many ways to save money in the Norwegian health care system, but deprioritising cancer treatment is not one of the methods that can and should be used. If the world's richest country can't afford to be the leader in developing future cancer treatment, who can? It is important that we as a nation contribute to more research, more development and better treatment. There is one reason why the health director’s play-out, to use the VM surgery, is a shot far above the target. The reason why we have to bet on experimental treatment is that it is what will eventually create the future cancer treatment. Developments in the cancer field are fast-moving, and Norway has to stick with it. If we are to look at the pillars that we have in Norwegian cancer research, where the Radium Hospital is the one, it is essential that what needs to be in place to achieve the goal of improving cancer treatment is to take care of the environment of competence that we already have. So this is the Radium Hospital. It is therefore important to ensure that the environment around the Radium Hospital, which has a good reputation internationally, is strengthened, as the opposition has called for several times in the last year. The cancer register is also an important pillar, which, in my opinion and in the view of the Progress Party, must remain as an independent unit, and not fragmented or rotated in relation to the management systems. The registry has a central role — and has had it — in Norwegian cancer research, and is perhaps one of the main reasons why we are in the forefront. This needs to be developed into more quality records for cancer. Of course, they should be pseudonymous, externally encrypted and based on consent or reservation requirements. The opposition has proposed this. Oslo Cancer Cluster, OCC, too, is an important pillar of cancer research. They have been a pioneer in the sense behind the integration of industry, research and treatment, a mindset that has become a model for many around the world – in fact, after just the Norwegian model. Therefore, both financially and not least under framework conditions, the OCC must be continued and strengthened so that they can remain the pillar they have been. In the end, I want to mention the Stamcell Center as the last pillar I'm pointing at. Here is much of the future in Norwegian and international cancer research. I personally believe that the answers are in this area. The Progress Party and the Workers' Party have fought through the establishment of the Stemcell Centre, but unfortunately we are far behind the corresponding country in terms of appropriations and resources for the work done – even Sweden is far ahead of us. It has to end. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3657	We live longer and longer, and an important condition to be able to enjoy living longer is that we can get needed health care when the need arises. In the same way that tuberculosis, or consumption as it was hot on the mouth of the people, hit many people about a hundred years ago and torn away people of their best age, is well cancer of our time's most terrifying disease. If you stand high or low in society, you don't get away. Even our dear King has had the diagnosis. The research has brought us closer to the answers to the causes of cancer. We know far more now than before, but we can't say that we've solved the cancer riddle. The BCG vaccine and the pirquet test gave us control of the tuberculosis, so it was almost eradicated here in the country for a period of time. Whether it's a benign tumor that stops growing after a while, or a malignant that continues to grow, appears to be part of the lottery of life. We know that malignant cancer cells can be transported through the bloodstreams and the lymphatic system and thus spread and form new tumours, or metastases as it is called, elsewhere in the body. Cell poisoning, hormone treatment, radiation treatment and surgery are the most common treatments for cancer, and predictions are best if the previous cancer is detected. Every third Northman will have the diagnosis cancer some time in his life. But of course, it's also doing a lot of good and groundbreaking work. What's important is to emphasize that we're all unique individuals. We're not just a number in a row. We're not just a diagnosis. We're not just a breast, a gland or a sick organ. We are people with thoughts and feelings, and we all have families and friends who are affected when the disease strikes. I have on several occasions brought forward the hospice philosophy with its holistic human vision. We know that there are ideological opposites to the establishment and operation of such units, but I have a hope that we could all agree that the very philosophy behind it, namely the approach to the whole human being, should be superior. In my opinion, it's only when the patient and the patient's best that's in focus, that interdisciplinary and interaction gets a real content. It's about quality of life even when you're sick – and maybe especially then. In this case, the opposition promotes several important proposals in order to strengthen its response to cancer.
