We know that about half of the cancer patients are referring to such treatment. It is important in relation to the patient’s experienced quality of life. It should therefore form an important part of the healthcare professionals’ obligation to provide patient and caregivers with good information about different methods. There is therefore a need to strengthen the knowledge base in this field as well. So I said initially that we're living longer, and that means that more people are at risk of getting cancer. It is therefore frightening when we lack research data on the elderly who get cancer. 85 pst. of all cancer cases, males over 55 years of age and 76 pst. in women. Exclusion of older people from research protocols leads us to know far too little about how older people tolerate treatment and respond to different methods. We know, for example. that many men die with prostate cancer, but they do not necessarily die of prostate cancer. Substantial changes are needed here, so that priorities can be made on a evidence-based basis rather than on an age scale. We need a reinforced response to cancer, and the proposals that are being promoted here today are good in this way.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3767	Today, the size deals with proposals from representatives for better legal certainty for patients. The proposal relates to specific legal aspects of the large and important complex of patient safety, namely that patients who have been asked by the Health Care Professional Act § 55 and the Patient Rights Act § 7-4 to assess the existence of a breach of the Health Care Professional Act are not currently to be regarded as a party to the case and are therefore neither given access to the documents of the case nor have the opportunity to appeal against the Health Care Authority’s decision. It is important that all aspects of patient safety are raised, so that we can improve patient safety and save many lives and avoid much suffering. I would like to begin by stressing that an overall committee believes that the work on improving patient safety is a major and important area of health policy and should be given priority. The Committee has had open consultation, where the FFO of the disabled has promoted the organisation’s views. The JRC has expressed to the Committee that the proposal for a representative meets some of the shortcomings and weaknesses of patients and their relatives in matters affecting possible violations of duties under the Health Care Act. Furthermore, the JRC has stated that lack of transparency and access to justice are a problem of legal certainty. An overall committee emphasises that the issue of the patient’s party position in such cases, inter alia: need to be assessed in relation to general legal perception, legal certainty, trust in healthcare, protection of confidentiality-related information, privacy, legal security aspects for healthcare professionals and the proper use of resources. The Committee refers to letters from the Minister of Health and Health of 12 May 2010 and is pleased to note that patients and relatives will be heard and taken seriously. The Committee notes that the Council of State has entrusted the State Health Authority with the task of setting up a call-out group to strengthen the supervisory review when serious incidents have occurred in the specialist health service. A single committee believes that a working group should be set up to review the practice of current legislation, including an assessment of the need for further statutory rights of complaint and opinion and greater transparency of documents. The Committee considers interventions that improve patient safety to be important and calls for a review of the practice of current legislation to be given priority. Health services are available to heal, relieve and comfort. Despite this, there are too many people who experience adverse events, many who are suffering pain, permanent loss of function, and there are many who die. That is why this is a major issue for healthcare and a major issue of health policy. The knowledge base on adverse events in Norwegian healthcare is not good enough. We do not have good figures from Norway on the damage and death caused by health services. The OECD has studied unavoidable deaths in health services and found that about 5 000 deaths in Norwegian health services may belong to this category. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3788	There was a paradigm shift in the health and social field in Norway in the 1990s. From a problem perspective with a focus on the negative – on the lack and on what makes people sick – we got a resource perspective that demands what makes people healthy, and what abilities each individual has to move towards a better health and a life with meaning for themselves. In other words, it is a more optimistic model that has faith in the individual’s self-maintenance and ability to master and develop. We received the Patient Rights Act as a consequence, which was adopted in 1999. As a patient, one is no longer a piece in the hands of specialists, but has clear rights under the Patient Rights Act to be thoroughly informed, could be re-assessed and, on a qualified basis, could give his consent to treatment. This is also followed up by regulations and circulars highlighting user involvement and self-determination as rights at system and individual level. The right to an individual level was also laid down in the law gradually. Last year, the change entered into force for persons who lack consent and who oppose the necessary health care. Fortunately, the law requires reliable measures to have been tried before going to use force and force. This is, of course, a major challenge with the efficiency requirements of the healthcare system facing today, and with a large number of nursing staff in small fractional positions. Confidence measures require gradual convergence and the patient is confident that what happens is transparent and recognisable, thus reducing the resistance to treatment. The distribution of healthcare is in an interaction between political and economic governance, medical judgement and legal control. The rating fixation changes the dynamics of this interaction. Legal control is strengthened through complaints and possible cases before the courts. Persons with legal rights will have to be given priority before patients who can also be helped, and this is precisely one of the purposes of providing certain patients or patient groups with a legal requirement for services. But where do patients’ rights stand today? We are all familiar with the waiting list issue, but it is not close to explaining why only 87 out of a total of 10,951 deadline breaks were reported to HELFO in the second tertiary in 2009. In other words, barely eight per million of those who did not receive treatment within the deadline reported. What are the causes here? The size is the legislative assembly and the granting authority, and it says that we should get little involved in individual matters. Despite that, it is often the fate of individuals who take much of our time in everyday life, and which makes us both agitated, upset, happy or sad. That's part of the ombudsman role. I have two issues that I want to raise, which highlight the importance of strengthening patients' legal certainty. The first concerns a man who suffered from periodontitis, and based on the information I have, did not receive a proper treatment of a dentist who even deleted with major drug problems. The dentist burned all the records and escaped from the country. Back there was a toothless patient. He was then informed by the authorities that the dentist was a private practitioner, so he had to go to civil proceedings for possible damages. The patient was in contact with the county council, county council, department, directorate, patient injury student and civil ombudsman without being able to get help. Now, eight years after he was referred to this dentist, and four years after the dentist left the country, the county council admits that the dentist had a contractual relationship with the county. The patient then tries to raise the case again, but is informed that the case has expired. I asked the Council of State in writing on 25 March this year about this relationship, and I am still waiting for an answer. Another tragic story is a meeting of the Progress Party fraction had with a parent killer with a boy who had suffered a brain injury, probably as a result of an operation. I invited the members of the Health and Care Committee to this meeting on 8 June, but it was only the Progress Party that had the opportunity to meet them. Here we have two parents who deliver a seemingly healthy boy who has problems hearing poorly because of a fluid build-up in his ear. Even the day after the operation, parents notice that something is wrong, but they do not get the help they feel the boy needs. Eight months after the operation, the parents ask that MRI be taken by the boy's head, because they suspect that he has suffered brain damage during the operation. The case is in the Patient Damage Study, which gives the specialist a limited mandate, so that the boy is never examined. The parents go many rounds with the hospital, which does not admit that something has gone wrong. The parents are trying to get a police report, but the police refer to the Health Department. The Health Authority will not raise a case because the purpose of raising a case is that the health service should learn from the error, and since the incident was four years behind in time, it is not relevant. Parents demand the legal certainty of their boy. It has been established that the boy has suffered a severe decline in development in the near post-three year olds. It was a crying father who told us that we were the first authorities to want to see the boy. It seems that the system is for the sake of the system, and not for the patient. I am therefore pleased that the Minister of Health is now opening up to ensure that patients and relatives are able to obtain party rights and make their contributions. We are waiting in excitement for how to ensure this in practice.

Firstly, of course, it is unimaginable and painful to lose their closest ones, and especially since in these cases this could have been avoided by proper treatment and monitoring. Secondly, they describe the next experienced betrayal, that is, how those who are affected feel that the system does not take them seriously, and that they have to fight to be heard. I therefore understand the background to the proposal very well. Although the vast majority of patients in Norway today experience safe and high-quality treatment, there are errors and unfortunate health service events that lead to death and lasting damage. Work to improve patient safety is a major and important area of health policy to be given priority. In this work, it is important that patients and relatives are taken seriously. It is particularly important when there may have been errors in health care treatment. We have to recognize and appreciate the fact that patients and relatives in such cases often have substantial information to come with. Therefore, it is very good that the Council of State has entrusted the State Health Authority with the task of setting up an expression group to strengthen the supervisory review when serious events have taken place in the specialist services. In addition, the Council of State notifies that they are to introduce a probationary system for two years with the obligation to provide a warning to the State Health Inspectorate when serious events have occurred. The purpose of the creation of a printing group and the warning obligation of serious events is to contribute to a better and faster assessment of the serious events. In this way, the supervisory authority may quickly enter into dialogue with those involved and quickly obtain an overview of what has happened, ensuring a secure collection of relevant information. It is also particularly positive that a joint committee should be set up to review the practice of today's legislation. The working group shall assess the need for additional legal remedies and expressions and greater transparency of the documents. However, while complaints are being made in the future, it is all the more important that the State’s health authorities work to improve their own practices, so that fewer patients and relatives feel the need to complain about the decisions. The State Health Authority has a very central task in Health-Noreg. Thorough and accurate supervision of what is happening in hospitals and other institutions is a provision for a proper health care system. In order to ensure patient safety, it is important to strengthen the possibility of effective control by the Health Authority. The Council of State points this out in a letter to the Committee dated 12 May. It refers to the State Health Authority’s objective of reducing as much as possible differences in treatment in the state through clear, professional management of supervisory activities. It shall be done by means of clear instructions in procedural guides, better training, more guidance, a list of precedents and audits at county offices, etc. Putting patient safety on the political agenda I think we can't do enough. Now there have been positive proposals recently on patient safety. Among other things, today we can read about a proposal that has been sent out on a hearing that applies to health professionals eight hours before they go on duty. Patient safety is a work that must be done in several fields over a long time. But I think keeping a political focus on this is necessary to ensure progress and improve it. I thought it was very positive that the Right should present this proposal for a representative.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3848	It is an important and good proposal that the Right has made. I would also like to commend the case rapporteur, who, in my opinion, got very serious in this matter. There have been a number of recent headlines in the media about serious injuries and unnatural deaths in hospitals. This affects many. It affects patients, relatives, health professionals, the hospital, the police and the health services. It also affects the population and their trust in hospitals. Hospitals have traditionally been characterised by little openness, including: by virtue of hierarchical organisation, protection of one another and reference to professional secrecy. In recent years, there has been a raging medical trend. In some areas, margins have become smaller, advanced equipment and techniques require knowledge, and hospital departments have a higher rate of pace. Therefore, the work on patient safety is one of the areas that needs to be very high priority in order to ensure confidence in healthcare as a whole and in healthcare professionals. In several meetings I have heard from relatives who are frustrated and despaired at the meeting with the healthcare department after unfortunate and serious events have occurred. They say that they do not receive any complaints or concessions, information is withheld, they feel that the matter is covered and that essential information is omitted in reports and records, deadlines are broken, etc. Work needs to be done in many areas to prevent patients and their relatives from meeting healthcare in this way. The proposal we are discussing today relates to one of the areas that ensure that relatives can be heard and involved to a greater extent when mistakes and unfortunate events occur. This will contribute to a more open healthcare system. Substantial changes in health care attitudes must be made. It's human to fail. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e3871	It is important that patients and relatives are heard and taken seriously. I also think that over time it increases the political contempt because we're seen as being superiors who just sail over, while the heroes that clean up are the ombudsmen. It is our duty to represent those who have voted for us. It is our duty to hear the unpleasant stories, read the unpleasant e-mails and get hold of it. That's how we contribute to creating a better society. If we outsource our ombudsman role, a lot of the point is that we're here. Then we can be a practical business ministry that only follows the role of the ombudsman. I think I want the politicians back to be the heroes, the ones who fix up. When people put their heads in the system, when people face injustice, it's our job to sort things out. When I go home today, it's my job to talk to those who are under the bridge down at Bjørvika, and ask what's going on to make their lives better. If we outsource it from this hall, we undermine the very pillars of democracy.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e4076	It is good that the proposers are committed to creating a decent and good living conditions for people with developmental disabilities. This was the cross-political agreement when the responsibilities reform was carried out in 1991, and I expect that there is still broad support for this value base, both at large, in the government and in the local government of the country. However, we need to keep an eye on how people with developmental disabilities are doing in different contexts. The reform provided for the termination of special care and institutions. People with developmental disabilities shall be provided with the necessary arrangements and assistance in the ordinary administrative and service equipment. Based on the same reasoning, a separate ombudsman system for people with developmental disabilities would be incorrect. Rather, we need to look at how the systems we have can work best for those with developmental disabilities. The patient representative for specialised healthcare users has been extended from September 2009 to the provision of health and care services in municipalities. This means that all users of municipal health and care services can receive free assistance from the Ombudsman. The Ombudsman also has the possibility to propose concrete measures on improvements and to inform supervisory authorities of critical circumstances. Because the Ombudsman is now working on matters from the entire healthcare system, it has changed its name to the Patient and User Ombudsman. The scheme, which has been in existence for several years, shall be evaluated after three years. The government will pay particular attention to the functioning of the scheme for people who are unable to express their own service needs. The majority of the Committee also asks the Ministry to consider whether sanctions should be strengthened. The user organisations say that today's complaint and supervision systems do not always work well enough. As a supervisory authority under the Social Services Act, the county has sanctioning powers in the form of being able to impose sanctions. According to reports to the State Health Authority, this sanctioning authority is very rarely used. This may be due to the fact that the county officials report that the municipalities follow-up when there is a lack of supervision. As the Board of Appeal, the Board of Appeal has inter alia: possibility of repealing or revoking local authority decisions. State health oversight revised the guidance for handling complaints under the Social Services Act in 2009. In cases where decisions on services are not complied with by the complainant, the county council is recommended to obtain a statement from the municipality and, where appropriate, to establish an incident-based supervisory case to see if the municipality has done what it can to provide the recipient with adequate assistance. The government is open to look at legislation and other measures that can enhance legal certainty for people with developmental disabilities. It's important that the big processes we're doing have a positive impact on this group. I am thinking in particular of the interaction reform, the new health and care law for the municipalities and the follow-up to the care plan 2015. Proposals are also concerned with the legal certainty of those with disabilities in criminal matters. This falls within the responsibility of the Minister of Justice, as many have been involved. The Ministry of Justice points out that mental retardation needs need to be identified as early as possible in the process. This means that efforts cannot be limited to the time when the person is under the responsibility of criminal law. I would also like to refer to the follow-up of the Ministry of Justice by means of investigations and projects which are briefed in the letter from the Minister of Health to the Committee. We cannot allow the policy that was drawn up in the context of the reform of responsibility to be included in the forgotten book. Therefore, it is commendable that the Minister for Children and Gender Equality has initiated a cross-ministerial information and development programme for development-impaired policy, as described in the Ministry of Children and Gender Equality’s budget proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-15.ana.ud2482e4121	Unfortunately, as the draft representative refers to, many people with disabilities do not receive the follow-up they require under different legislation. Although a patient ombudsman system has been established to provide for the follow-up to health and care, it is now time for developmentally disabled people to experience unworthy treatment in different areas of society. This also applies in the school sector, one of the areas that the patient representative is not responsible for following up. Today, many people with developmental disabilities feel that they are not being trained in line with the Training Act. The 2009 Supervisory Communication shows that the supervision of compliance of school owners with the requirements of adapted training and special education shows deviations up to 90 pst. There is no doubt that this is in the interests of the legal certainty of the students, and that inadequate follow-up every day helps to create school losers. The periodic penalty payments or fines are possible penalties in the event of failure to comply with the obligations imposed by the county year after year. There is ‘reason to believe that some municipalities speculate in breaking the law’, says Assistant Secretary General Jarl Ovesen of the Municipal Report on 3 February 2010. The organisations of disabled people will have more state control over the municipalities and stronger sanctioning options. It requires both the Joint Organisation of Disability and the Norwegian Association of Devolved Persons. Today it is the state through the county that detects crimes in the field of education, but they are not followed up. Many of the crimes happen because municipalities and counties have to save money, and sacrifice the weakest in our society to get it done. An example of this is that many set up too large teaching groups, so that students are not given the opportunity to receive special education according to their own abilities and assumptions, as stated by the Training Act. When, year after year, the county discovers the same offences without any punishment for municipalities and county councils, it may seem that the crime is tacitly accepted against those with developmental disabilities. The journal ‘Society for All’ shows in an article on illegal provision of training in secondary school that many parents find it too complicated to complain. The Progress Party clearly believes that there is a need for a separate representative for those with developmental disabilities, an agent who can deliver an overall offer in terms of the rights of those with developmental disabilities — and end the offences that are detected year after year and that also occur within the school system — in line with several other areas of service.
